@@2386403 <p> Shale gas exploration could lead to water contamination because wells may leak underground , a study backed by the British Geological Survey has warned . <p> More than six per cent of wells in a major shale exploration region in Pennsylvania have reported some kind of leak , analysis by independent academic group Researching Fracking in Europe ( ReFINE ) found . <p> The scale of leakage from existing oil and gas wells in the UK may be underestimated because of a lack of data and monitoring , according to the report by academics from the BGS , Durham University and Duke University . <p> The finding is a blow to the Government , which wants to encourage fracking for shale gas in an attempt to lower energy bills and has insisted that the controversial process is safe . <p> Professor Richard Davies of Durham University , one of the report 's authors , told the Telegraph : " It is a small risk that a well leaks and this has been proven in the United States . " <p> Fracking involves drilling a well deep underground @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at high pressure to fracture the rocks and enable shale gas or oil trapped within them to flow out . <p> Mr Davies said an earlier paper by ReFINE had showed the fracturing process itself was " highly unlikely " to cause contamination because the fractures were too deep underground to reach water supplies . <p> But he said the new study , published on Tuesday , showed that problems with the structure of the wells -- such as inadequate cement seals - could lead to gas or oil leaking out . <p> " A few years ago people were saying fracking causes water contamination . We have robustly researched that ... and we have not found any evidence that fracking is the problem , " he said . " It 's the boreholes that could cause water contamination , and emissions into the atmosphere . " <p> " Shale gas requires a lot of wells to be drilled ; more wells to produce the same volume of gas from shale as from a conventional reservoir . That 's why well integrity is critical . " <p> The study found that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in 2000 , one had leaked . But it found this was " likely to be an underestimate of the actual number of wells that have experienced integrity failure " because of lack of data . <p> Mr Davies said : " Because it has happened in the past , it is a small risk for the UK . Unfortunately we ca n't be clearer on that , because there is n't data in the public domain from active wells and no-one is monitoring abandoned wells . " <p> A total of 2,152 wells were drilled for oil and gas onshore in the UK between 1902 and 2013 , but very little drilling has so far been carried out for shale gas . <p> The UK Onshore Operators Group , representing the oil and gas companies , said the report should " give great reassurance about the quality of operations and regulation in the UK onshore oil and gas industry " because it showed a " very low rate of issues with respect to operational wells in the UK " . <p> " We are in the process of agreeing with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as recommended in the report , " it said . <p> A spokesman for the Department of Energy and Climate Change said : " ReFINE 's report highlights just how important well construction is as part of safe and environmentally sound exploration . Experts will also consider all aspects of the design and construction of wells , including how they will be made safe after they are no longer in use . " <p> Lawrence Carter , Greenpeace energy campaigner , said : " Government ministers and shale gas executives consistently argue that we do n't need to worry about fracking as we 've been drilling onshore for years . @@2386415 <h> In his new book , Tiny Stations , Dixe Wills visits Britain 's tranquil railway request stops , each of which has a fascinating history . Here he picks his 10 favourites <p> Who has n't felt an agreeable sense of power when holding out a hand to stop a bus ? Imagine then how much more pleasing it is to put your hand out and have a whole train stop for you . If you think Britain affords no opportunity for such exploits then think again -- the nation sports around 150 railway request stops dotted about from Cornwall to the far north of Scotland . Little used and often shorn of their original purpose by the onward rush of the years , these stations are typically havens of tranquillity where visitors can do a little casual time-travelling to a Britain all but forgotten . <h> Burnley Barracks , Lancashire <p> It 's something of a miracle that , 156 years after it was opened , Burnley Barracks station is still with us . Originally named Burnley Westgate , it was intended as a temporary terminus @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ after four months ' doughty service , the station was closed as planned . However , these were the busy days of Empire and along came a cavalry barracks next door . The station was hastily re-opened and re-named . The most notable cavalryman to pass bandy-legged through its doors was James Yorke Scarlett , best known for leading the Heavy Brigade at Balaclava . Unlike that of its doomed lighter brother , the Heavy Brigade 's charge met with success and Scarlett became a national hero . A campaign in 2008 for the station to be re-named again came to nothing , which is sad because Burnley Barack Obama has a nice ring to it . <p> Pity Reddish South for it is served by a single solitary train each week . Passengers wishing to visit the station must wait for Friday and board the ' Denton Flyer ' -- the 10.13 service from Stockport to Stalybridge . Those who miss it face a 168-hour wait for the next train which , even by British standards , is quite an interlude . Worse still , if you 're stricken by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ north you 'll have to charter your own train . To add insult to infrequency the station does n't even get a proper mention in the timetable , appearing merely in footnote F to Table 78 . A visit is well the worth the trouble though for the place is kept immaculate by the Friends of Reddish South Station and , beyond the flower beds , a bright and bold mural bears life-affirming aphorisms . <h> Lympstone Commando , Devon <p> All along the platform at Lympstone Commando passengers are assailed with signs reading , ' Persons who alight here must only have business with the Camp . ' Since the camp in question is a commando training base , this is not a warning to take lightly . However , having sought permission in advance to alight here you 'll witness a rare sight : a railway station whose exit is under surveillance from a sentry box ( well , a sort of sentry office ) . Also , when you tell the guard you 'd like to get out here there 's a chance you might be mistaken for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is likely to pep up your day . Although the camp was established in 1939 the station itself only arrived 37 years later , sparing Britain 's finest the half-mile walk from Exton station . This means they can save their energies for the assault course , clearly visible from the platform and quite clearly impossible . <h> Bootle , Cumbria <p> Shockingly , there is no plaque on Bootle station to commemorate the heroism of driver Harold Goodall and fireman Herbert Stubbs . On 22 March 1945 , Goodall 's train was passing Bootle when he noticed a fire in a wagon containing depth charges . He stopped a safe distance away and he and Stubbs uncoupled the burning wagon from the ones behind it . Goodall drove forward and the pair returned to uncouple the wagon from the rest of the train , taking the remaining wagons up the line . Stubbs ran forward to prevent any other traffic from passing but Goodall approached the wagon a third time to try to extinguish the flames . He was killed instantly when the depth charges detonated . The explosion left @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ away . One can only imagine the devastation had all seven wagons containing depth charges gone up . Harold Goodall and Herbert Stubbs , we salute you . <h> Sugar Loaf , Powys <p> Of the Heart of Wales line 's 32 stations , no fewer than 17 are request stops , with Sugar Loaf the least requested of all . Yet it has not allowed its never more than tenuous raison d ' ? tre to get it down -- even though its single platform is half-buried in an obscure cutting miles from the nearest village it remains an exceptionally cheery station at which to spend time . Named after a nearby hill which is n't even the famous Sugar Loaf ( that 's in Monmouthshire ) , it was built to serve four railway workers ' cottages , the foundations of which can still be made out . The station closed in 1965 , only to be re-opened 19 years later to cater for ramblers and cyclists . So be warned , it can get a bit hectic : nowadays Sugar Loaf receives an average of five passengers a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Hartley famously observed , ' The past is a foreign country : they do things differently there . ' If you need proof take a trip to Conwy station on the north Welsh coast . The eastern approach , through Robert Stephenson 's tubular railway bridge with its fake towers that all but merge with Conwy Castle , would be impressive for a terminus , let alone a request stop . Then just as the line enters the station , it passes through a conveniently huge arch in the ancient town wall . Except it was not a conveniently huge arch -- Stephenson just sent his railway crashing through the wall which , back in those days , was only a little over 550 years old so could practically be considered new-build . Conwy has since been declared a World Heritage Site so one hopes no one will be smashing any more of it down any time soon . <h> Talsarnau , Gwynedd <p> As the effects of climate change take hold , we 've become used to seeing images of parts of Britain under water . That said , you @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ confident it will be some time before anyone mentions Wales . However , if you 'd been living in Talsarnau in 1927 ( I make the bold assumption here that you were not ) you 'd have spoken of little else , for that was the year the tsunami struck . Sweeping up the Dwyryd Estuary from the Irish Sea on 28 October , the freak wave ripped through the village and tore up railway lines . The locals stoically rebuilt their community , transforming it into a popular seaside resort for a while . Their station is on land that was actually part of the sea until the early 19th century , so if you visit do keep an eye out just in case Neptune returns to claim it again . <p> Talsarnau station and Penychain station ( see following entry ) are on a section of the Cambrian Coast Railway ( arrivatrainswales.co.uk ) that is currently closed because of flood damage ; a temporary bus service will operate until the line reopens in May . <h> Penychain , Gwynedd <p> The old and never less than hilarious joke about @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ camps . This , as we know , is not true . For example , take the Pwllheli camp , in the midst of which Penychain station stands . Billy Butlin designed and built it not as a prison camp at all but as a training centre for the navy called HMS Glendower . After the war , Butlin was allowed to buy the place and open it as a holiday camp . Come the 50s , most of the weekly intake of 8,000 holidaymakers came by train to hear resident acts such as Rory Storm and the Hurricanes with one Richard ' Ringo ' Starkey hitting the skins . The camp is now owned by Butlin 's sister company ; Rory Storm is long dead ; and most visitors arrive by car nowadays . It 's a sad world indeed . <h> Duncraig , Ross-shire <p> Drug dealers get a bad press -- few people warm to them and almost everyone 's happy when they go to prison . There was a time though when drug dealing was the most respectable of professions . Take , for example , Victorian @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Matheson Group . He made his staggering fortune selling opium to the Chinese before returning home to his native Scotland to build himself Duncraig Castle , enter parliament and gain a knighthood . When the line from Inverness to the Kyle of Lochalsh opened in 1897 Matheson had Duncraig station built as his castle 's own private halt , adding a hexagonal waiting room that aped one of the rooms in the castle . The waiting room is still in situ and beautifully preserved , while the station 's single diminutive platform enjoys views of Loch Carron so exhilarating you can probably leave your supply of opium at home . <h> Dunrobin Castle , Sutherland <p> Though completely remodelled in 1845 , Dunrobin Castle ( below ) is one of Scotland 's oldest inhabited houses , having been the seat of the Earls and Dukes of Sutherland -- one of Scotland 's seven ancient earldoms -- since 1235 . Today 's magnificent fairytale confection is the work of Sir Charles Barry , the architect best known for knocking off the Houses of Parliament . However , lovers of more dainty attractions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ eponymous station which , like that at Duncraig , began life as a private halt ( where a former Duke of Sutherland kept his own private train , as one does ) . There the fortunate visitor will encounter a wonderful black-and-white Arts and Crafts construction oozing workmanship from every finial . Originally serving as the waiting room it now houses a small museum of railway memorabilia . With just three trains each way passing through daily , passengers have plenty of time to enjoy it . The station will hold an open day on May 12 , from 11.30am to 5pm ; a steam train will stop there for 90 minutes from 1.30pm to 3pm . @@2388893 <h> Exposing the thorium myth <h> Nuclear enthusiasts have been singing the praises of nuclear reactors that use thorium as their fuel instead of uranium . Jan Ber ? nek analyses the claims - and finds that thorium is a mere distraction on the way to our renewable future . <p> It 's awfully late to be thinking of developing a whole new fuel cycle whose problems differ only in detail from current versions . <p> Both were named after ancient gods : Uranus was the principal Greek god of the sky while Thor was the Norse ( and broadly Germanic ) god of thunder . <p> There 's a modern mythology surrounding thorium and uranium too , such as the currently very popular suggestion that thorium can replace uranium and deliver much better ( safer , cheaper , fuel abundant ) nuclear energy . <p> We 've heard it all before <p> Well , we 've heard all these things from advocates of nuclear energy before , have n't we ? Were n't we told in the 50 's , 60 's , 70 's , 80 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ these miracles would actually be delivered by uranium fueled reactors ? <p> Yeah , something obviously went wrong because none of those dreams actually came true , despite half a century 's worth of effort and hundreds of billions in subsidies poured into the nuclear industry . <p> What are the chances that replacing the Greek god with a Germanic one will help ? Would Thor take his powerful hammer and nail it all down ? Not likely . <p> It 's still a fission technology <p> Thorium technology is based on nuclear fission and therefore keeps fission 's inherent problems . It partially addresses one of the downsides of current commercial reactors based on the uranium / plutonium fuel cycle . <p> That one downside is the production of ( mostly unwanted ) plutonium and other long-lived transuranic isotopes . The thorium cycle does indeed produce much less of these . So far , so good . <p> However thorium retains all the other main hazards - in fuel mining and fabrication , reactor safety , production of dangerous waste , and last but not least , nuclear weapons proliferation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " With uranium-based nuclear power continuing its decades-long economic collapse , it 's awfully late to be thinking of developing a whole new fuel cycle whose problems differ only in detail from current versions . " <p> Let 's look more closely at some of the hopeful claims around thorium . <p> Thorium is abundant , uranium is running out <p> It 's absolutely true that uranium reserves are limited , and that the world is turning to ever lower-grade , and more expensive to process ( in money and energy ) uranium ores . It 's also true that thorium is abundant and ' cheap as chips ' - it 's produced as a waste product of rare earth mining . <p> But the benefit is not what it 's claimed to be . The uranium ' shortage ' applies only to fissile U-235 . However the world is awash with non-fissile U-238 as the leftovers of uranium enrichment . The U-238 is however ' fertile ' - it can be transmuted into fissile plutonium-239 under neutron irradiation in a reactor . <p> And that 's exactly what governments and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to turn into a viable nuclear fuel cycle for six decades in so called ' fast breeder ' reactors . <p> And in this respect thorium is exactly the same . Thorium-232 is non-fissile , but fertile , and can be transmuted into fissile uranium-233 under neutron irradation in a reactor . Until you do that , it is to all intents and purposes inert . <p> And given that all over the world , U-238 / Pu-239 fast breeder reactors have proved to be technical and economic disasters , do we really want to bet the farm on making them work with thorium ? <p> Safer reactors ? <p> The risks inherent in nuclear reactors are due to the massive concentrations of radioactive materials and the huge amount of heat they produce ( which is actually needed to generate electricity ) . <p> No matter if the fuel is based on uranium or thorium , if it 's solid or liquid , this characteristic alone will inevitably continue to be the Achilles heel of any nuclear reactor . <p> " the choice between uranium-based fuel and thorium-based fuel is seen @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in addressing the nuclear power issues ... " <p> Less nuclear waste ? <p> It 's obvious that fission applied to different nuclear fuel results in a different composition of radioactive waste . But it 's still radioactive waste and whether the waste produced by thorium reactors is really less problematic ( because there 's little or in an ideal world no plutonium in it ) remains an open question . <p> Spent thorium fuel still contains long-lived isotopes such as proactinium-231 ( with a half-life 32,000 years which is even longer than plutonium Pu-239 ) which implies the need for long term management in timescales comparable to typical high level waste from uranium reactors . <p> Not surprisingly , a chart published in Nuclear Engineering International magazine in November 2009 shows that the radiotoxicity of spent thorium fuel is actually higher than uranium spent fuel over the long term , ie after first 10,000 years . See image top-right <p> No proliferation ? <p> Yes , thorium ca n't itself be used to build nuclear weapons but it ca n't be used directly as a nuclear fuel either . In @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fissile uranium isotope , U-233 . <p> And that 's an isotope that is suitable for nuclear weapons . Thorium enthusiasts claim that the inevitable presence of the highly penetrating gamma emitter U-232 as a contaminant would make this impossible . <p> However the US successfully detonated a nuclear bomb containing U-233 in 1955 in the Nevada Desert as part of its Operation Teapot - the so called MET ( Military Effects Test ) shot . <p> The UK Department of Energy and Climate Change commissioned a report which concluded in 2012 that the claims by thorium proponents who say that the radioactive chemical element makes it impossible to build a bomb from nuclear waste , leaves less hazardous waste than uranium reactors , and that it runs more efficiently , are " overstated " . <p> Another report on thorium finds that in an LFTR ( liquid fluoride thorium reactor ) , the continuous pyro-processing / electro-refining technologies intrinsic to the design ( if they ever actually work ) " could generate streams of U-233 very low in U-232 at a purity well above weapons grade as currently defined . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Thorium reactors exist only in blueprints and early experiments , which means there could be other issues not yet detected that would complicate their large scale implementation . <p> In any case , this also means that it would take much longer than a decade before thorium reactors would potentially become available for a larger commercial deployment . <p> Recent studies , like the one published by the Norwegian Thorium Commission , while being supportive of the concept , also conclude that there are many uncertainties and problems related to it . <p> It notes that Norwegian thorium reserves are of limited economic attractiveness compared to other sources ; that accelerator-based reactors will be viable in the distant future at best ; that thorium reactors would create nuclear waste problems ; and that any of this will require massive international research . <p> It can not compete economically with uranium <p> Related to this is thorium 's unknown economic performance . Experts suggest that one of the key reasons why thorium reactors are not being developed is that they can not compete economically with uranium fuel-based reactors . <p> This @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ characterising and optimising the uranium / plutonium fuel cycle - and similar sums would have to be invested all over again to do the same for thorium . Add to that the costs of more complicated fuel fabrication and processing . <p> And current pressurized water reactors are already uncompetitive . With investment costs of current reactor technology easily reaching $8,000 / kW of installed capacity , it is difficult to imagine that thorium reactors would be developed and built in foreseeable future . <p> But the PR is good ... <p> All in all , it rather looks like the nuclear industry , failing terribly to provide a reliable and affordable energy source , is trying to divert our attention from its scandals and incompetence to a distant , rosy dream . Not bad PR but not much else . <p> While we are once again told to dream about a bright nuclear future , modern renewable energy technologies are already cheaper and upscaled well beyond nuclear . <p> In 2013 , new nuclear capacity of only 4,000 MW was globally installed in four single reactors , but installations of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Those newly added capacities of wind and solar alone will generate , on an annual basis , as much electricity as 20 large reactors . <p> The future is renewable ! <p> Let 's not get distracted . Let 's not waste even more time , money and brainpower on trying to make the impossible : a nuclear energy source that would actually really work . The future is renewable , and that 's where we need to go as quickly as possible . <p> Jan Ber ? nek is the leader of Greenpeace International 's Energy Campaign . <p> This article was first published on Greenpeace 's Blog . It has been supplemented by The Ecologist , most importantly with the addition of one entire section : ' Thorium is abundant , uranium is running out ' .

" <p> " We have clear plans in place to meet our 2020 renewable energy target . We are making good progress . At 4.2 per cent the UK is less than a third of the way towards its target of having 15 per cent of all energy generated using renewable means . <p> The EU report showcases government listlessness since 2004 in regards to green energy and subverts Cameron 's claim that the coalition will be " the greenest government ever . " <p> Topping the bill is Norway , for whom 64.5 percent of energy is produced sustainably.

@@2391532 <h> The Extreme Life of the Sea by Stephen R. Palumbi and Anthony R. Palumbi : Book review <p> " Extreme " anything makes for a good selling title these days : a symptom of our jaded consumerist palates . But The Extreme Life of the Sea -- that 's another matter . There 's nothing forced about these creatures : they just exist , innocently , doing what they have to do . Stephen and Anthony Palumbi -- father and son ; biologist and science writer -- are brilliant guides to this realm about which we as a species have been remarkably incurious . <p> There have been exceptions . Darwin , one of the most enquiring men who ever lived , puzzled at the enormous creatures that live in the apparently empty oceans . What on earth sustains them ? My oceanography bible has always been Sir Alister Hardy 's classic two volumes on The Open Sea ( 1956 ; 1959 ) . From this I learnt about the photosynthesizing microplankton at the bottom of the food chain and the krill that feed on them , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Blue . But we know so much more now . The Palumbis p ? re et fils give us the new stories in succinct prose beautifully freighted with apt similes and metaphors : the Blackfin icefish ( whose environmentally friendly antifreeze proteins have been cloned and used in our ice cream ) has prominent lips , " making them look like moustachioed RAF pilots " ; in the patterns on the clownfish , " Nature holds a seminar on graphic design " . <p> The greatest revelation of recent research is that the ocean is dominated not by its giants but by its microflora and fauna : bacteria and viruses , its primary producers . We harvest about 88 million tonnes of fish and shellfish annually ; the ocean 's microbes produce this amount of biomass in about one hour . This is the missing link in those supposedly " empty " oceans that baffled Darwin . <p> A feature of life under water is the longevity of some of the creatures : it is oxygen that both sustains and limits the span of animal life and there is much @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ over 200 years , some fishes manage 100 plus and there is even an immortal : the Turritopsis jellyfish which , if stressed , can dissolve itself back to the larval form and start all over again . <p> The extreme deep conjures a picture of eternal dark and menacingly ugly and odd creatures . This is partly true but it is n't uniformly pitch black thanks to bioluminescence : living light generated by enzymes . Most of the light is blue-green and that is all most creatures can perceive but one fish can generate and see red light . It goes its way with a secret torch nothing else can observe : in the kingdom of the blind-to-red , the Stoplight loosejaw is king . <p> The oceans are our future and it is here that the drama of climate change will play out with the greatest consequences : if we destroy their ecosystems we will lose more than the food they provide . The complacent attitude excoriated by Philip Larkin in the poem ' Going Going ' -- " Chuck filth in the sea . . . The tides @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . We need the Palumbis ' dazzling little book to put us right . <p> Peter Forbes and Tom Grimsey 's Nanoscience : Giants of the Infinitesimal will be published by Papadakis in May 
@@2391579 <p> Arriving with maximum complication at its finale , the cop saga Line of Duty ( BBC Two ) was showered with praise by nearly all critics . But to join the chorus , surely you had to have enjoyed the long haul from episode to episode . <p> I , for one , always had doubts . How was it that the carefully pursed lips of DS Steve Arnott had pulled every attractive woman in or near the police station ? @@2391597 <h> South Korea <p> Jeong Ho-jin dons a pair of plastic gloves to show off his most proud achievement as a district official in Seoul , and then uses his keys to unlock a large , rectangular contraption that looks like some kind of futuristic top-loading washing machine . Loaded with bins half-filled with decomposing ginseng , lettuce and other meal remnants , this , it turns out , is South Korea 's high-tech solution to food waste . <p> Jeong works in one of two districts in Seoul where the high-tech food waste management program is being piloted . The program works by giving each household a card that has a radio frequency identification ( RFID ) chip embedded in it containing the user 's name and address . They scan their card on a small card-reader on the front of the high-tech bin to get the lid to open , then dump the food waste into the bin and onto the scale at the bottom , which gives a numerical reading of the waste 's weight and disposal cost . <p> " Before this everyone paid @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ throw their food waste away without thinking , " said Jeong . <p> The apartment complex Jeong took me to is like many in Seoul , made up of tall , grey cookie-cutter buildings . Everything is clean and orderly , save for the heaps of trash bags and food waste bins he 's thrilled that the foreign media are interested in learning about . Fortunately for both of us Seoul 's winter is hanging on into March for one last chill , so we do n't need to plug our noses as we chat over vats of discarded leftovers . <p> Each household is sent a bill for the total disposal costs at the end of the month . The bills are n't the type that we all dread finding in the mailbox . Residents are charged per kilo of waste discarded and officials say the average household ends up with a monthly bill of around 1,300 won ( about 72 pence ) . That is n't much , of course , but it will double over the coming year . Public services like utilities , waste disposal and transportation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Getting rid of food waste is a matter of particular urgency in South Korean households . Korean food tends to be fragrant , as much of it is fermented , an old custom for preservation through cold winters . Most Korean meals are based around some kind of soup , meaning after almost every meal there 's some amount of thick , pungent stew to dispose of . <p> South Korea also has a population of more than 50 million in a mountainous space smaller than Iceland . This population density makes proper waste handling extra important . Last year , the government started a nationwide program to charge residents according the weight of what they were throwing away , a change that officials hope will lead to a nationwide reduction in food waste . <p> The Ministry of Environment has said that the goal is to eventually reduce total national food waste by 20% , thereby cutting waste treatment costs . <p> " Before people could see the mess , but they could n't see the weight . Now they know exactly how much they 're throwing out @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to waste less , " said Kim Min-seob of Seoul government 's environment management division , the body responsible for food waste policy . <p> Officials in Seoul hope that the program will eventually be implemented throughout the country , but are facing problems with limited funding . The machines required for the system cost 1.75mn Korean won ( about ? 980 ) and have the capacity to serve 60 households . <p> The system will face a funding crunch this year . Costs for the program had been covered almost evenly by Seoul city government , the national government and the district , but the city-level funding is being discontinued after this year . <p> Officials will need to find a new source of funding before the program can be more widely implemented , though there are plans to expand it this year to Gangnam ( of the famously catchy Psy tune ) and Songpa , two of Seoul 's more upscale districts . <p> In the less affluent north of the city , Oh Bok-hee owns an apartment building where she acts as landlady and superintendent . Waste and excess were huge problems then and have grown only bigger since -- 50% bigger , according to Jonathan Bloom 's 2010 book on the subject , American Wasteland . <p> Americans , by many estimates , now waste more than 40% of their food , compared with a global average of around 30% . About 20% rots in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , or is rejected before it reaches the supermarket because of imperfections , many of them as trivial as a single browned leaf on a head of salad or a small bruise on an apple . <p> A lot more gets thrown out because expiration dates come and go -- even though the food itself , in many cases , is still perfectly good . A shocking amount gets stashed into overstuffed home refrigerators and forgotten behind the milk or the leftover Thai takeout until it stinks up the place and gets chucked days or weeks later . <p> There is no shortage of staggering statistics : a total of 387bn calories wasted every day , in a country where 50 million people struggle to get enough to eat ; discarded food accounting for 14% of the contents of American waste dumps and producing methane and other greenhouse gases ; millions of animals needlessly slaughtered ; at least 2% of the country 's total energy resources expended on uneaten food ; $100bn a year lost to the national economy . <p> " The supersize mentality is quite disgusting , " says @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Los Angeles that is attempting to point the culture in a different direction . " The food waste would be enough , but it 's not realistic to gather every single apple core and banana . "

@@2391683 <h> New Christian Aid report shows human cost of climate change <p> 27 March 2014 - Christian Aid today publishes a new report showing the devastating effects of climate change , with communities worldwide , particularly in worst hit poorer countries , being forced to change their way of life . <p> While record-breaking floods in the UK received massive media coverage , along with broad acceptance that climate change was to blame , the voices of those suffering even greater impacts have largely gone unheard . <p> Using personal stories from seven different countries ; Bangladesh , Brazil , Kenya , Malawi , El Salvador , Bolivia and the Philippines , the report Taken by Storm : responding to the impacts of climate change , reveals the stark reality of life as a result of extreme weather events such as drought and flooding . <p> The report is intended to put a human face to the latest findings of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change ( IPCC ) which on Monday will publish its latest report on the impacts of climate change . <p> Among other things @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ temperatures with less rainfall in arid regions . Child malnutrition is likely to be a serious risk as food quality and quantity is affected . <p> In a foreword introducing Taken by Storm , Dr Rowan Williams , Chair of Christian Aid , says : ' These are stories from some of the poorest people in the world . Those who suffer most acutely from the menacing effects of climate change , such as the quilombolas of Brazil , or the pastoralists in northern Kenya , are those who have done least to contribute to it ; and there is a deep injustice in this . <p> ' Among all the facts and statistics , the summits and debates , it is essential for us to remember the specific human faces of those who suffer because of climate instability . <p> ' Far from being a vague threat in the distant future , a warming world is very much a present reality , with global temperatures already having risen by 0.8 ? C since before the industrial revolution . Stronger storm surges , heavier rain , and scarcer resources are part @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , with far fewer resources to deal with it than we have in the developed world . ' <p> As well as showing the different ways people are experiencing a distorted climate , the Christian Aid report outlines some of the ingenious ways people are adapting their lifestyles to survive . <p> Measures range from farmers receiving weather forecasts by text message in Kenya , to the introduction of saltwater resistant ducks in flooded Bangladesh . Despite this resilience , the report is clear that adaptation measures offer only a temporary solution . <p> Report author Dr Alison Doig , Christian Aid 's Senior Climate Change Adviser , said it was crucial that politicians took heed of the clear warnings from both the international scientific community and those suffering first-hand . <p> ' People living on the front line of climate change are the canaries in the climate coalmine , ' she said . <p> ' But their plight is more than just a warning of what many other parts of the world can expect . <p> ' These are individuals paying the price for the actions of wealthy nations and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> ' It is vital that politicians hear their voices and heed the warnings of the IPCC and make tackling climate change a priority if we are to pass on a safe planet fit for future generations . ' <h> Notes to editors : <p> 1 . Christian Aid works in some of the world 's poorest communities in around 50 countries at any one time . We act where there is great need , regardless of religion , helping people to live a full life , free from poverty . We provide urgent , practical and effective assistance in tackling the root causes of poverty as well as its effects . <p> 2 . @@2394461 <p> The NGO landscape is restructuring : international NGOs are migrating south , expatriate placements are becoming rarer and some national operations in the global north are being shut down altogether . This is partly due to financial constraints . <p> Oxfam is one of the biggest NGOs currently undergoing a major restructure . Starting with the UK , it expects to cut 125 jobs and close UK offices outside Oxford headquarters . International operations will form the major focus over the next two years . Oxfam GB employs 5,300 people worldwide , working with more than 22,000 volunteers and seven national Oxfams . " It is imperative that we have an Oxfam which lives within its means and is relevant to 21st century needs , " said Oxfam CEO Mark Goldring when he announced the restructure last October . " Advances in technology mean we no longer need as much support in head office . Instead our resources will be focused in the regions where we carry out the majority of our work . " <p> Other NGOs are reshaping along similar lines . ActionAid led the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , in the mid 2000s . Save the Children is currently amalgamating its 20-plus national bodies into one single entity with shared back office functions ( pdf ) . There are major efficiencies to be found here , such as reducing duplication of HR , finance , logistics , procurement and IT . <p> Inherent within these announcements is a seismic power shift from the global north to the global south . Marianne Bo Paludan , programme manager at Save the Children Denmark says : " The value-added of northern NGOs like Save the Children working in partnership with southern CSOs civil service organisations is increasingly being questioned ... donors are beginning to fund southern CSOs directly , bypassing northern NGOs altogether and putting these organisations under pressure to reposition themselves " . <p> Much of this is to be celebrated , it shows that infrastructure to take on international NGOs work has been built in country . However it does leave international NGOs with some big questions about what its role should be . <p> Innovative new structures are emerging to answer those questions . Increasingly these cluster around the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of multi-party stakeholders or partners from civil society , government departments , indigenous communities , even the media , mostly located within the country where the development work is taking place . The NGO 's role becomes one of supporting capacity building and knowledge sharing among these stakeholders , rather than designing and implementing projects and programmes . <p> Typically a networked model involves decentralisation of management and a shift from headquarters in the global north to a back office in the north and operational divisions moving south . The recent restructure of children 's charity EveryChild offers an example of what a newly networked organisation looks like . Its CEO Anna Feuchtwang said : " Like many other INGOs we have a hierarchical structure with strategic decisions taken in the UK , approved by a board of British trustees which are then implemented through liaison offices overseas who deliver programmes . When we asked ourselves what we thought our most effective contribution to change might be we realised that our structure was upside down . " <p> EveryChild 's next step was to effectively wind down its programmes and any @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . It relaunched at the start of 2014 virtually unrecognisable , now a founder member of a growing international alliance . EveryChild effectively no longer runs any programmes under its own name , but rather will raise funds for the programmes delivered by other alliance members . <p> " We now see our role as an enabler to bring the voices of children and civil society organisations to the international arena , " said Feuchtwang . " We recognise that we have a place to play in mobilising resources , advocating for change with our own government and internationally , but that we are only a part of the picture . " <p> While this might be leading to an identity crisis among some international NGOs , Jonathan Glennie , research associate at ODI believes it does not spell their demise . " In the future there will be an increased need for international NGOs not a reduced need , but those organisations will be really different , they will do less service delivery , more networking ... It wo n't be from the north helping the south , they are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Africa . " <p> In a networked international NGO model , co-ordination becomes more important than ever . " The world has globalised , " says Glennie , talking from his home in Bogota , Colombia . " The problems that indigenous communities are facing here on the hills , that black communities are facing on the coast , are related to the international private sector , to changes in power , sustainable development issues , climate change -- you ca n't , if you ever could , deal with these problems just locally . The role of international advocacy , learning , sharing , consolidation of power ... That 's the role of powerful , global NGOs now . @@2394846 <h> Nuclear share approaches 20% of UK generation in 2013 <p> UK nuclear generation increased by 0.4% cent on 2012 , to 19.8% of the total UK generation in 2013 , according to preliminary figures released by the UK Department of Energy and Climate Change ( DECC ) . In 2013 , coal accounted for 36.3% of UK generation , with gas 26.8% , and renewables 14.8% . <p> The share of generation from both coal and gas fell year on year , with gas 's share declining from 29% in 2012 to 28.6% , its lowest level for 17 years . Renewables ' share of electricity generation increased from 11.3 % in 2012 to 14.8 % in 2013 . <p> Total electricity generated from nuclear in 2013 , totaled 70.6 TWh , up from 70.4 TWh in 2012 , according to the preliminary data . <p> Within the renewables segment , generation from onshore wind ( 16.5 TWh ) , offshore wind ( 10.9 TWh ) , solar PV ( 2.0 TWh ) and bioenergy ( 18.7 TWh ) all increased . However , generation from hydro @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ earlier , due to lower rainfall in catchment areas in 2013 . <p> Nuclear generation accounted for 19.7% of total electricity generated in the fourth quarter of 2013 , up from the 17.2% share in the fourth quarter of 2012 , due to increased availability , DECC said . <p> Electricity generated in 2013 also fell by 2.0% , from 363.8 TWh in 2012 to 356.7 TWh . DECC 's preliminary figures also indicated that electricity consumption was 0.5% lower than in 2012 and at its lowest level since 1998 . <p> The preliminary statistics can be downloaded from the DECC website . @@2396553 <h> Casper Henderson finds the legendary nature writer as amazed as ever by the countryside <p> On the way to the Crown Inn at Burston , Richard Mabey stops the car to point out the Burston Strike School . The modest building beside a neat Norfolk village green has not been in use for many years but it is cherished by radicals and nonconformists across the country , who assemble in a yearly commemoration of a time , between the First and Second World Wars , when rural working people withdrew their children from a Church of England school controlled by the wealthy local elite . For Mabey , it speaks of a spirit of stubborn independence that he has celebrated throughout a long career asone of England 's foremost writers about natureand the countryside . <p> I have come to see Mabey , who lives outside Diss and not far from Burston , on the eve of the publication ofDreams of the Good Life , his biography of Flora Thompson . Thompson 's best-known work , Lark Rise to Candleford , a numinous but unsentimental trilogy about @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 19th century , has been a national classic almost since it was published during the Second World War . But Thompson herself has remained unknown , and Mabey is keen to emphasise that she was not some untutored child of nature but a woman from a working-class background for whom success came only after 40 years of struggle . <p> Mabey , 73 , is in vigorous form and loving life in Norfolk , where he moved a dozen years ago after the episode of depression he documented in his memoir Nature Cure ( 2005 ) . Growing up in the hills around Berkhamstead , Mabey dwelled among trees and books . The writings of Richard Jefferies , a visionary of nature , were an early love and one to which he returned in the Eighties when he edited an anthology of Jefferies 's work . Studying PPE at Oxford at the time of the Cuban missile crisis in 1962 was a political awakening . Mabey joined the Committee of 100 , an anti-war group set up by Bertrand Russell and others committed to non-violent resistance and civil disobedience . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ political nightmares of that time , was never far away . While working as an editor at Penguin , he wrote Food for Free ( 1972 ) , a guide for foragers of wild berries , fungi and shoreline delights . The book was an immediate success and has never been out of print . " It is my pension fund , " he says with a smile . Mabey 's second book , The Unofficial Countryside , helped to define a new kind of nature writing . It was republished in 2010 with an introduction by Iain Sinclair , the doyen of British psychogeographers . <p> The 30 or so books Mabey has written over the past 40 years are diverse , but perhaps one common theme is that nature and the non-human is stranger , more various and more surprising than we tend to assume . This sometimes goes for those who write about nature too , as Mabey showed in his biography of Gilbert White , a man who was much more than a simple country parson and diarist . Elegy and soft focus have little place in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in a stinging riposte from Mabey to an essay he published last July in which Poole accused Britain 's nature writers of bourgeois escapism . <p> Mabey is not afraid to take a heterodox position . Though concerned about climate change and the mass extinction of species , he reminds me that sometimes plants and animals can be more resilient than we anticipate . As we drive by the thickest carpet of snowdrops in years , he recalls that less than 20 years ago scientists were warning that the early spring flowers would be gone by now . Last year Mabey was among the few -- alongside the historian of the countryside Oliver Rackham -- to suggest that the fear that ash trees would be exterminated by a new fungal infection was overblown ; it was to underestimate the adaptability and , for want of a better phrase , " long intelligence " of these organisms . On this spring 's floods , he is delighted that the Environment Agency has " come out of the closet intellectually " on the need to rethink land-use patterns and embrace such measures as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ no signs of slowing down . His next book , probably to be titled " Moonflowers : Adventures in Romantic Botany " , is " not about how humans have used plants , but how at critical moments in all kinds of societies ' and individuals ' histories including my own , plants have transformed the way we see ourselves " . He wants , he says , to challenge our views of the nature of time , flow and beauty . The book will range from ancient chimera such as the Green Man to extraordinary recent discoveries of sense perception and information processing in plants . Mabey looks forward to a fellowship at Emmanuel College , Cambridge -- where his friend the nature writer Robert Macfarlane is a don -- and access to the University Botanic Garden . <p> Back at the 16th-century cottage where he lives ( with his partner , Polly ) Mabey takes me around the garden , which is carefully cultivated near the house and progressively wilder as one moves away from it . His writing shed overlooks a small pool . For centuries hemp was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " retted " , or soaked , in pools like this in order to separate the fibres , which were to be used in clothing , from the husks . In Nature Cure , Mabey describes this pool , as a " mute oracle at the juncture of the human and non-human worlds , prodigiously deep and blank-faced ... it is frequented by sparrowhawks , woodpeckers and at least six species of dragonfly . A mysterious cold spring bubbles up beneath it . " Far from mute or cold , Mabey is something of an oracle himself .

@@2397780 <p> For centuries , explorers have searched for fabled new trade routes through the frozen waters of the Arctic that would speed up commerce across the globe . <p> Climate change , which has thawed more of the Arctic 's ice cap for longer periods during the summer months , could be about to slash the time it takes for cargoes to reach their destinations and transform trade patterns . <p> The UN 's Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change ( IPCC ) is expected to reveal in a closely watched report this week that warmer sea temperatures by 2050 could open up new channels across the Arctic Northern Sea Route ( NSR ) . According to the report , global warming could mean that at current rates the NSR will remain open to shipping for around 125 days per year , up from an average of about 50 days at the moment . <p> " Increased shipping associated with the opening of the NSR will lead to increased resource extraction on land and in the sea , and with two-way commodity flows between the Atlantic and Pacific , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ environmental blogs over the weekend . <p> The majority of traded goods and raw materials are still transported globally by ship through the Suez and Panama canals , which link east with west . Since 1970 , seaborne trade has increased by an average of 4pc each year , according to official US data . <p> The NSR is one of two main channels which currently exist through the Arctic , which have the potential to cut the distance those large freight vessels and tankers have to sale from European ports through the Suez Canal and on to China by up to 40pc . At the end of the Cold War in the early 90s the former Soviet Union allowed international shipping to use the NSR , which is increasingly being looked at as a viable sea route to Asia . <p> The other major route across the Arctic seas and the more difficult to navigate is the North West Passage , which meanders through ice packed north of Canada . The frozen channel separates the Atlantic with the northern Pacific Ocean . Sir Francis Drake tried and failed to find @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ when the Norwegian Roald Amundsen made it through entirely by sea that the NWP was entirely conquered by ship . <p> Beyond having the potential to speed up the delivery of large cargos of raw commodities such as coal , iron ore and grain these Arctic routes are now seriously being considered by energy companies seeking faster ways to deliver liquefied natural gas ( LNG ) . <p> Gas is transforming global energy markets but the major challenge still remains of how best to transport the fuel produced in remote regions to densely populated markets . Pipelines running across borders are the cheapest option but as Russia 's recent row with the West over Crimea proves such conduits are vulnerable to political turmoil . <p> Shipping gas that is first frozen to liquid form on-board giant tankers is strategically the most flexible solution and the least vulnerable to political risk . <p> According to a BG Group report " shipping LNG through the NSR or , less likely , the NWP has the potential to significantly reduce times and transportation costs for some suppliers . " <p> The first LNG shipment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Norway to a power company in Japan in 2012 . According to BG Group , the journey reduced the normal sailing time by 10 days and saved $1m in charter day rates . However , LNG tankers still require special icebreaking escorts to make the Arctic journeys and although gas carriers have been designed with stronger hulls none have so far been commissioned . <p> According to BG Group significant challenges remain despite the thawing ice : " While some believe that the Arctic waters will slowly open up as climate change increases the availability of ice-free waters , in the medium term specialised shipping will be necessary along with crews experienced in transiting these waters . " <p> Credit Suisse has revised up its outlook for oil prices after initial expectations at the beginning of the year for a rapid increase in production from Libya and Iran failed to materialise . <p> The broker has reset its target for Brent crude to between $100 ( ? 60 ) a barrel and $120 after predicting that oil would average lower prices this year . <p> " We expect to see tight @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ our new oil price forecast looks similar to last year 's trajectory , " said the broker in a recent note to investors . " Market sentiment has already shifted from profoundly bearish to neutral , but we think that sentiment could swing to outright bullish . " <p> A lack of progress in the lifting of sanctions on Iran , which could lead to a rapid rise in production , was a major factor in the broker 's analysis . Libya 's continued problems were also cited as a factor with political unrest continuing to disrupt Tripoli 's export potential . <p> " We no longer count on large increases in exports from Libya and Iran , " said Credit Suisse in its report . <p> However , the broker 's forecast could still be challenged by Iraq 's continued increases in production . @@2399647 <p> Pensioners left on their own during a heatwave in industrialised countries . Single mothers in rural areas . Workers who spend most of their days outdoors . Slum dwellers in the megacities of the developing world . <p> These are some of the vulnerable groups who will feel the brunt of climate change as its effects become more pronounced in the coming decades , according to a game-changing report from the UN 's climate panel released on Monday . Climate change is occurring on all continents and in the oceans , the authors say , driving heatwaves and other weather-related disasters . <p> And the changes to the Earth 's climate are fuelling violent conflicts . The UN for the first time in this report has designated climate change a threat to human security . <p> The overriding lesson of this report , the scientists said , was that unless governments acted now to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and adopt measures to protect their people , nobody would be immune to climate change . <p> " There is n't a single region that thinks we can avoid @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ adaptation -- let alone 4 degrees , " said Dr Rachel Warren of the Tyndall centre for climate change research at the University of East Anglia . <p> " I think you can say that in order to keep global temperature rises at 2 degrees we need to reduce emissions greatly and rapidly , but even at 2 degrees there are still impacts that we ca n't adapt to . " <p> " We live in an era of manmade climate change , " said Dr Vicente Barros , who chaired the report . " In many cases , we are not prepared for the climate-related risks that we already face . Investments in better preparation can pay dividends both for the present and for the future . " <p> But those who did the least to cause climate change would be the first in the line of fire : the poor and the weak , and communities that were subjected to discrimination , the report found . <p> Scientists went to great lengths in the report to single out people and communities who would be most at risk of climate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> " People who are socially , economically , culturally , politically , institutionally or otherwise marginalised are especially vulnerable to climate change , " it said . <p> One impact is through the reduction in crop yields , which leads to higher prices . " The story is that crop yields have detectably changed . As time goes on the poor countries that are in the warmer and drier parts of the planet will feel the crop yield decreases early , " said Michael Oppenheimer , professor of geosciences and international affairs at Princeton University . " When you get above two degrees and into the three- and four-degree range , adaptation becomes less effective and even some of the wealthy countries that have advanced agriculture start suffering . " <p> " People who were already disadvantaged , more of them are going to be suffering from malnutrition , " he added . <p> In a further cruel twist , the report said climate change would also make it harder for developing countries to climb out of poverty , and would create " poverty pockets " in rich and poor @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , director of the Red Cross climate centre and an author of the report , said the agency was already seeing evidence that the poor were being hit hardest in weather-related disasters . <p> " It 's the poor suffering more during disasters , and of course the same hazard causes a much bigger disaster in poorer countries , making it even poorer , " he said . <p> There are already more weather-related mega-disasters such as heatwaves and storm surges occurring under climate change . <p> And the number of natural disasters between 2000 and 2009 was around three times higher than in the 1980s , Van Aalst said . " But it does have a hand in producing situations that lead to conflict . <p> " The things that drive conflict are sensitive to climate , particularly poverty and economic shocks , " Adger said . " It 's more likely to be more local and more site-specific . " <p> And it could set back efforts to deal with climate change . " Conflict itself actually reduces the ability of places to react to climate change , " Adger added . " The impact of conflict in destabilising regions , wiping out infrastructure , not allowing the state to fulfil its social contract to protect its own people ... conflict itself is making people more vulnerable to climate change . "

@@2399844 <h> War in the Modern World <h> Facing the crisis : from global change to insurgency <p> Guest post by Clement Roy . Clement is interested in both geosciences and socio-political issues . He got a BSC. in applied physics and a MSc. in Environment at University of Geneva . He then worked in the field of energy , agriculture , and mining , and carried out a year of research around the world in geodynamics and paleoceanography ( at Geotop of Montreal and University of Tokyo ) , before serving as an officier in the Navy French . He is now affiliated to the Institut de Physique du Globe of Paris as well as the French Institute of Geopolitics where he works respectively in the field of natural hazard and Asian piracy . <p> Dear Kings of War readers . This is my first post here , and I would like first to thank David Betz for giving me the opportunity to contribute . As a guest- french scientist in the field of natural science , and former military- I am here to answer his ' On @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Interdisciplinary studies , crossing natural sciences , anthropology , and archeology , provide examples of ancient societies subject to environmental challenges . The Maya civilization , after reaching its demographic peak , collapsed between 750 and 900 . In less than a century , the regional population shifted from three million to about four hundred thousand . Different assumptions about the collapse have been advanced : internal ( or social ) causes , or climate , as an external cause . Climate change could be the primary driver , affecting all the others , leading to a chain reaction , causing vegetation shift , and difficulties in the supply and management of water . In this context , conflicts between cities , so wars for ultimate resources were favored . <p> We can therefore make a link between environmental stress and political disorders . We learn through the example of the Maya , that the maximum exploitation of the natural environment , resource dependence , and natural environmental variability led their civilization to a very vulnerable position . Using the example of the Maya , and those , among others , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at which we could add , more recently , Nauru and Kiribati , Jared Diamond ( in " Collapse " ) give us examples of societies coping with troubles , successful or not , with adaptive mechanisms according to their cultural values . <p> We also learn that if an environmental crisis occurs , it becomes quickly systemic and leads to economic and social aspects . According Joseph Tainter , anthropologist and historian , complexity is a natural way in the evolution of societies . Every adaptation , after problem-solving , complicates the society . But this complexity comes along with structural rigidity and therefore vulnerability when an unforeseen event occurs . The French system , very centralized , leaving little autonomy to local communities , although it may react quickly , is indeed particularly vulnerable to long-term crisis . <p> Humanity 's wealth depends on the diversity of culture it contains . But they can not be thought apart from their growth substrate and environment . ' Humanity is a piece of the world ' , wrote Friedrich Ratzel in his ' Anthropogeography ' . He knew that well , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the founders of ecology . Ecosystems are not discrete spaces ; they are continuum . A continuum that species , including humans , can ionize , thus modifying more or less strongly . Indeed , any organization tends to change its environment in order to increase comfort , therefore its existence . But ultimately , this pressure creates a general level of stress to the ecosystem , which changes by a threshold effect . <p> Yet if environmental changes are continuous phenomena , our coping strategies crystallize on a discrete spectrum of solutions , often extreme . Ecologists become more radical ; while governments want certainty before moving . We may observe this in the discourse on climate change : media bias , and positions cluster around on a favourite side . People expect a ' solution ' , while the necessary adaptation to this phenomenon is rather a way for us to redefine ourselves . But the truth is that we are adaptable only when our identity is strong enough , that is to say when our representation of our place in Nature is as strong as possible . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a motion appears , from societies or Nature , people will either migrate elsewhere to find their former comfort , or struggle to re-establish the world they use to know in their physical environment . <p> Let 's propose here a draft concept . In a structural environment , the pioneer species is still subject to the environment and adaptation . The strength of the hazard is still structuring in a society , because of their inability to raise capital to erect a protection . This species may still be dominant , but it does not yet change the substance of the ecosystem . To conquer is not to submit . After a certain degree of control , the substrate of growth becomes obedient , i.e. , it is the species that become structural for it . They then put pressure on the environment allowing it to create such a barrier , placing them ' outside Nature ' in an artificial environment which maximizes comfort . The ' Anthropocene ' is such an environment for humans : it buffers the potentially adverse and unexpected natural variations . And its ecosystem of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the City . <p> We therefore propose a two-axis analytical grid : ( i ) the importance of structuring , and ( ii ) kinetics of change . That means four types of situations , or four types of representations of Human position in Nature . But only one , the first , corresponds to a maximum comfort for us . <p> 1- Obedient and stable Nature , as in the City , which allows the development of our species while avoiding the most uncomfortable situation . <p> 2- Structuring and stable natural environment , as in the countryside . <p> 3- But when the environment is dynamic , harmony disappears ; the Man-Nature balance of power appears . By a natural disaster , such as an earthquake , if Nature has the initiative . <p> 4- Or when population tries to re-appropriate their environment in a fluid context of insurgency and irregular warfare . In this case , it is an attempt to regain control against change in the substance of their ecosystem . <p> We should pay particular attention to current developments of Human-environment interactions and strategies in a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cohesion , sense of responsibility , and finally democracy are no longer favored . And when we all share the same plate , spit inside becomes a way of appropriating it : thus begins the race and wars for the last resources , to lose nothing of our competitiveness and inaction when it comes to cope with pollution . <p> So , here I would like to answer David Betz 's post ' On strategic neo-Catastrophism ' . It is not only conflict between nations that are to be expected . Conflicts inside the Nations may also be favoured . In 1979 , Hans Jonas released its ' responsibility principle ' . What has become today ? A rather vague principle , which leaves great flexibility in industrial activities , without making citizens more proactive . If governments become as inert as civil society , it is normal that a response emerges from non-governmental organizations and local communities . <p> Remember the great Frank Herbert science fiction novel ' Dune ' , adapted by David Lynch as a film in 1984 : ' who can destroy a thing , controls that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ oil wells during the 1990 Gulf War , local population , in their struggle to re-appropriate their environment , would prefer to rather destroy it than abdicate to what they deem to be an external , imperialist , and dehumanized interference <p> Standard <h> Post navigation <h> One thought on " Facing the crisis : from global change to insurgency " <p> Please let me know if you 're looking for a article writer for your site .
